\forall k\in\{i,j\},\|G_{k}-F_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq\alpha\end{subarray}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G_{i},u)+\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(G_{j},u)\right\}>0\>.

Further lower bounding by a finite number of strictly positive constants yields the result.
∎

For all
i\in[K]
, we define the distributions for which
i
is among the best arm

\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i}:=\left\{\bm{F}\in\mathcal{F}^{K}\mid i\in i^{\star}(\bm{F})\right\}\>.

Lemma 31
.

Let
i^{\star}\in[K]
,
\bm{F}\in\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}
,
i\neq i^{\star}
and
\varphi\in[0,1]
. Define for
\beta\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle G_{i}(\bm{F},\beta)

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\beta\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+\varphi\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\sup_{w\in\triangle_{K}:w_{i^{\star}}=\beta}\min_{j\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{j}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{j},u)\right\}\>.

Then,
(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto G_{i}(\bm{F},\beta)
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}\times[0,1]
. Moreover, the function
\bm{F}\mapsto\inf_{\tilde{\beta}:|\beta-\tilde{\beta}|\leq\xi}G_{i}(\bm{F},\tilde{\beta})
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}
.

Let
\bm{\nu}\in\mathcal{F}^{K}_{1}
,
\bm{F}\in\mathcal{F}^{2}
such that
m(F_{1})>m(F_{2})
,
\alpha>0
and
\varphi\in[0,1]
. Define for
\beta\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle H(\bm{F},\beta)

\displaystyle=\alpha\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\beta\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{1},u)+\varphi\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{2},u)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\sup_{w\in\triangle_{K}:w_{1}=\beta}\min_{i\neq 1}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left[w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(\nu_{1},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\nu_{i},u)\right]\>.

Then,
(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto H(\bm{F},\beta)
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{2}\times[0,1]
. Moreover, the function
\bm{F}\mapsto\inf_{\tilde{\beta}:|\beta-\tilde{\beta}|\leq\xi}H(\bm{F},\tilde{\beta})
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
\bigcup_{i\in[K]}\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i}=\mathcal{F}^{K}
, it is enough to show the property for all
i\in[K]
. Let
i^{\star}\in[K]
and
i\neq i^{\star}
. In the proof of Lemma
58
, we have obtained that

\displaystyle(\bm{F},w)\mapsto\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}

and

\displaystyle(\bm{F},w)\mapsto\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}

are continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}\times\triangle_{K}
.

Let
\Phi_{i^{\star}}:(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto\{w\in\triangle_{K}\mid w_{i^{\star}}=\beta\}
, it is compact valued and non-empty for all
\beta\in[0,1]
. It is also continuous (both lower and upper hemicontinuous). Using the continuity proven above, Berge’s theorem yields that

(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto\sup_{w\in\triangle_{K}:w_{1}=\beta}\min_{i\neq 1}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left[w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(\nu_{1},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\nu_{i},u)\right]\>,

is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}\times[0,1]
. Combining with the above continuity results, we obtain that
(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto G_{i}(\bm{F},\beta)
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}\times[0,1]
for all
i^{\star}\in[K]
, hence on
\mathcal{F}^{K}\times[0,1]
.

Let
\Phi:\bm{F}\mapsto\left\{\tilde{\beta}:|\beta-\tilde{\beta}|\leq\xi\right\}
, it is a continuous (constant), compact valued and non-empty correspondence.
Using the continuity proven above, Berge’s theorem yields that
\bm{F}\mapsto\inf_{\tilde{\beta}:|\beta-\tilde{\beta}|\leq\xi}G_{i}(\bm{F},\tilde{\beta})
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}
.

Using exactly the same arguments, we obtain that
(\bm{F},\beta)\mapsto H(\bm{F},\beta)
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{2}\times[0,1]
and
\bm{F}\mapsto\inf_{\tilde{\beta}:|\beta-\tilde{\beta}|\leq\xi}H(\bm{F},\tilde{\beta})
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{2}
.
∎

Appendix E
Concentration

In Appendix
E.1
, we leverage results on martingales
[
5
]
to prove
\delta
-correctness of the threshold (
4
) from Lemma
2
.
In Appendix
E.2
, we derive technical results needed in our analysis of Top Two algorithms based on concentration for sub-Gaussian random variables.

E.1
Calibration for bounded distributions

After proving Lemma
32
, we give a threshold for bounded distributions (Lemma
2
).
The concentration is obtained as a direct corollary of recent work on martingales
[
5
]
.
We apply their technical result to the case of bounded distributions.

Calibration by concentration

Lemma
32
states that
\delta
-correct thresholds can be obtained by concentration results.

Lemma 32
.

If with probability
1-\delta
, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})
,

N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i})+N_{n,i^{\star}(\bm{F})}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,i^{\star}(\bm{F})},\mu_{i^{\star}(\bm{F})}))\leq c(n,\delta)\>,

(36)

then the stopping rule (
2
) using
c(n,\delta)
is
\delta
-correct on
\mathcal{F}^{K}
.

Proof.

Let
i^{\star}=i^{\star}(\bm{F})
and
\hat{\imath}_{n}=i^{\star}(\bm{F}_{n})
. The empirical transportation costs in (
1
) can be rewritten as

W_{n}(\hat{\imath}_{n},j)=\inf_{x\leq y}\left[N_{n,\hat{\imath}_{n}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,\hat{\imath}_{n}},x)+N_{n,j}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,j},y)\right]\>.

Using
j=i^{\star}
,
x=\mu_{i}
and
y=\mu_{i^{\star}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\tau_{\delta}<+\infty,\hat{\imath}_{\tau_{\delta}}\neq i^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\exists n\in\mathbb{N},\>\exists i\neq i^{\star},\>i=\hat{\imath}_{n},\>\min_{j\neq i}W_{n}(i,j)>c(n,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\exists n\in\mathbb{N},\>\exists i\neq i^{\star},\>N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i})+N_{n,i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,i^{\star}},\mu_{i^{\star}})>c(n,\delta)\right)\>.

∎

Concentration of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}

The key technical result, which was extracted from
[
5
]
, is reproduced in Lemma
33
.

Lemma 33

(Lemma E.1 in
[
5
]
)
.

Let a compact and convex set
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
q
be the uniform distribution on
\Lambda
. Let
g_{t}:\Lambda\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be any series of exp-concave functions. Then,

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda}\sum_{k=1}^{n}g_{k}(\bm{\lambda})\leq\log\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\lambda}\sim q}\left[e^{\sum_{k=1}^{n}g_{t}(\bm{\lambda})}\right]+d\log(n+1)+1

We are now ready to prove Lemma
2
.

Proof.

For all
(n,i)\in\mathbb{N}\times[K]
, we denote by
(X_{k,i})_{k\in[N_{n,i}]}
the samples collected on arm
i
.
Let
i^{\star}=i^{\star}(\bm{F})
and
i\in[K]\setminus\{i^{\star}\}
.
Using Theorem
3
, we obtain

\displaystyle N_{n,i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,i^{\star}},\mu_{i^{\star}}))

\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\in\left[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu_{i^{\star}}}\right]}\sum_{k\in[N_{n,i^{\star}}]}\log(1-\lambda(X_{k,i^{\star}}-\mu_{i^{\star}}))\>,

\displaystyle N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i}))

\displaystyle=\max_{\lambda\in\left[0,\frac{1}{\mu_{i}}\right]}\sum_{k\in[N_{n,i}]}\log(1+\lambda(X_{k,i}-\mu_{i}))\>.

Let
q_{i}^{+}
and
q_{i}^{-}
be the uniform distributions over
\left[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu_{i}}\right]
and
\left[0,\frac{1}{\mu_{i}}\right]
, which are compact and convex sets of
\mathbb{R}
. Define

\displaystyle L_{n,i}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda\sim q_{i}^{-}}\left[\prod_{k\in[N_{n,i}]}(1+\lambda(X_{k,i}-\mu_{i}))\mid X_{1,i},\cdots,X_{N_{n,i},i}\right]\>,

\displaystyle U_{n,i}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda\sim q_{i}^{+}}\left[\prod_{k\in[N_{n,i}]}(1-\lambda(X_{i,k}-\mu_{i}))\mid X_{1,i},\cdots,X_{N_{n,i},i}\right]\>,

\displaystyle Y^{-}_{n,i}

\displaystyle=N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i})-\log(N_{n,i}+1)-1\>,

\displaystyle Y^{+}_{n,i}

\displaystyle=N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i})-\log(N_{n,i}+1)-1\>.

With
d=1
, using Lemma
33
with the exp-concave functions
g_{k,i}^{+}(\lambda)=\log(1-\lambda(X_{k,i}-\mu_{i}))
for
k\in[N_{n,i}]
, and
g_{k,i}^{-}(\lambda)=\log(1+\lambda(X_{k,i}-\mu_{i}))
for
k\in[N_{n,i}]
, yields

\displaystyle e^{Y^{-}_{n,i}}\leq L_{n,i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad e^{Y^{+}_{n,i}}\leq U_{n,i}\qquad\text{a.s.}

Furthermore, it is easy to verify that for each arm
i\in[K]
,
L_{n,i}
and
U_{n,i}
are non-negative martingales with unit initial value
L_{0,i}=1
and
U_{0,i}=1
almost surely.
The martingale property is shown directly by the tower rule (conditioned on the arm sampled at time
n
) and
\mathbb{E}\left[1\pm\lambda(X_{N_{n,i},i}-\mu_{i})\right]=1
.
Furthermore, they satisfy
\mathbb{E}[U_{n,i}]\leq 1
and
\mathbb{E}[L_{n,i}]\leq 1
.
Thus,
U_{n,i^{\star}}L_{n,i}
is a non-negative martingale with unit initial value.

By concavity of
\log
and using
\sum_{j\in\{i,i^{\star}\}}N_{n,j}\leq n
, we have

c(n,\delta)\geq\log\left(\frac{K-1}{\delta}\right)+2+\sum_{j\in\{i,i^{\star}\}}\log\left(N_{n,j}+1\right)\>.

Taking a union bound over
i\neq i^{\star}
and using Ville’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\in\mathbb{N},\>\exists i\neq i^{\star},\>N_{n,i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n,i},\mu_{i})+N_{n,i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n,i^{\star}},\mu_{i^{\star}})>c(n,\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\neq i^{\star}}\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\in\mathbb{N},\>Y^{-}_{n,i}+Y^{+}_{n,i^{\star}}>\log\left(\frac{K-1}{\delta}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\neq i^{\star}}\mathbb{P}\left(\exists t\in\mathbb{N},\>U_{n,i^{\star}}L_{n,i}>\frac{K-1}{\delta}\right)\leq\delta\>.

Combining the above concentration with Lemma
32
yields the result.
∎

E.2
Sub-Gaussian random variables

We want to exhibit a sub-Gaussian random variables which controls the deviation of various random variables to their means. More precisely, we will prove the existence of
W_{1}
in Lemma
5
and
W_{2}
in Lemma
14
.

Definition 3
.

A random variable
X
is said to be sub-Gaussian with constant
c
if for all
x\geq 0
,
\mathbb{P}(X\geq x)\leq e^{-cx^{2}/2}
and for all
x\leq 0
,
\mathbb{P}(X\leq x)\leq e^{-cx^{2}/2}
.

We are interested in sub-Gaussian random variable mainly due to the following property.

Lemma 34
.

If
X
is sub-Gaussian, then for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda X}]<\infty
.

The proof can be found in any textbook dealing with sub-Gaussian random variables, e.g.
[
42
]
. We will furthermore use the following classical properties:

•

If
X
and
Y
are sub-Gaussian and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
then
X+Y
is sub-Gaussian and
\alpha X
is sub-Gaussian.

•

Bounded random variables are sub-Gaussian.

•

If
X
verifies that for all
x\geq x_{1}\geq 0
,
\mathbb{P}(X\geq x)\leq a_{1}e^{-c_{1}x^{2}/2}
and for all
x\leq x_{2}\leq 0
,
\mathbb{P}(X\leq x)\leq a_{2}e^{-c_{2}x^{2}/2}
, then
X
is sub-Gaussian.

•

The maximum (or minimum) of a finite number of sub-Gaussian random variables is sub-Gaussian.

Lemma 35
.

If
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 1}
are sub-Gaussian random variables with constants
(c_{n})
such that
\inf_{n}c_{n}>0
, then
\sup_{n}\frac{X_{n}}{\sqrt{n\log(e+n)/(1+n)}}
is sub-Gaussian.

Proof.

For
x\geq\sqrt{\frac{8}{\inf_{n}c_{n}}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\xi\geq x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(X_{n}\geq x\sqrt{\frac{n\log(e+n)}{n+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-(\inf_{n}c_{n})x^{2}{\frac{n\log(e+n)}{n+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left[2\log(e+n)+\frac{\inf_{n}c_{n}}{2}\frac{x^{2}n}{n+1}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left[2\log(e+n)+\frac{\inf_{n}c_{n}}{4}x^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(e+n)^{2}}\right]e^{-\frac{\inf_{n}c_{n}}{4}x^{2}}\>,

where we have used that
\frac{n}{n+1}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}
and that
\alpha\beta\geq\alpha+\beta
for
\alpha,\beta\geq 2
. Now for the lower tail, for
x\leq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\xi\leq x)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(X_{1}\leq x\sqrt{\frac{\log(1+e)}{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\log(1+e)}{2}c_{1}x^{2}/2\right)\>.

∎

Application to our work

We will use the following two examples of sub-Gaussian variables. Lemma
36
is a consequence of the Dvoretzky-Kiefer-Wolfowitz (DKW) inequality
[
31
]
while Lemma
37
follows from Azuma’s inequality.

Lemma 36
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be i.i.d. random variables with cdf
F
and let
F_{n}
be the empirical distribution function of
(X_{i})_{i\in[n]}
. Then for all
n
,
\sqrt{n}\|F_{n}-F\|_{\infty}
is sub-Gaussian with a constant which does not depend on
n
.

Lemma 37
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a martingale with mean 0 and
c
-sub-Gaussian increments and
\mu_{n}=X_{n}/n
. Then for all
n
,
\sqrt{n}\mu_{n}
is sub-Gaussian with constant
c
.

Theses results permit to establish the concentration results that are used in Appendix
C
and
D
.

Lemma

(Lemma
5
and
14
)
.

There exists sub-Gaussian random variables
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
such that for all
(n,i)\in\mathbb{N}\times[K]
with
n\geq K+1
(such that all arms are pulled at least once)

\displaystyle|N_{n,i}-\Psi_{n,i}|\leq W_{1}\sqrt{(n+1)\log(e+n)}\quad\text{a.s.}\>,

\displaystyle\left\|{F}_{n,i}-F_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq W_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(e+N_{n,i})}{1+N_{n,i}}}\quad\text{a.s.}\>.

In particular,
\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda W_{i}}\right]<+\infty
for all
\lambda>0
and
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

For the first inequality, we use that
N_{n,i}-\Psi_{n,i}
is a martingale and combine Lemma
37
with Lemma
35
to get that for all
i\in[K]

W_{1,i}:=\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{|N_{n,i}-\Psi_{n,i}|/\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{n\log(e+n)/(1+n)}}

is sub-Gaussian. Therefore
W_{1}=\max_{i\in[K]}W_{1,i}
is sub-Gaussian and we have, for all
(n,i)
,

|N_{n,i}-\Psi_{n,i}|\leq W_{1}{\sqrt{\frac{n^{2}}{n+1}\log(e+n)}}\leq W_{1}\sqrt{(n+1)\log(e+n)}.

For the second inequality, we define
W_{2,i}:=\sup_{n\geq K+1}\left\|{F}_{n,i}-F_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{1+N_{n,i}}{\log(e+N_{n,i})}}
. Letting
\hat{F}_{n,i}
be the empirical distribution fo the first
n
samples from arm
i
(while
F_{n,i}
is the empirical distribution of the samples collected up to time
n
), one can rewrite

W_{2,i}=\sup_{n\geq 1}\left\|\hat{F}_{n,i}-F_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\sqrt{\frac{1+n}{\log(e+n)}}=\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{\sqrt{n}\left\|\hat{F}_{n,i}-F_{i}\right\|_{\infty}}{\sqrt{n\log(e+n)/(1+n)}}.

Combining Lemma
36
with Lemma
35
yields that
W_{2,i}
is sub-Gaussian for all
i\in[K]
. Then
W_{2}=\max_{i\in[K]}W_{2,i}
is sub-Gaussian and we have, for all
(n,i)
,

\left\|{F}_{n,i}-F_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq W_{1}\sqrt{\frac{1+N_{n,i}}{\log(e+N_{n,i})}}.

∎

Appendix F
Kinf for bounded distributions

Here,
\mathcal{F}
is the set of probability distributions with support in the interval
[0,B]
.
The goal of this section is to study the properties of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
, which are functions
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]>\mu\}\>,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]<\mu\}\>.

As a first step, we remark that for
\mu\in(0,B)
we can rewrite the
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}
functions using non-strict inequalities, which will be more convenient
[
18
,
17
]
. We do so and will work in this section on

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\geq\mu\}\>,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\leq\mu\}\>.

There is a strong link between these two definitions, which we will use to transport results from one function to the other.

Lemma 38
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}
,
\mu\in[0,B]
and let
f:[0,B]\to[0,B]
be defined by
f(x)=B-x
.
Let
F^{B-X}
be the pushforward measure of
F
through
f
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{B-X},B-\mu)=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F^{B-X},B-\mu)=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)\>.

Proof.

The function
f
is measurable, bijective and involutive. We have
\mathrm{KL}(F,G)=\mathrm{KL}(F^{B-X},G^{B-X})
for all
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{B-X},B-\mu)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F^{B-X},G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\geq B-\mu\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F^{B-X},G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G^{B-X}}[X]\leq\mu\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F^{B-X},(G^{B-X})^{B-X})\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G^{B-X}}[X]\leq\mu\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G^{B-X})\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G^{B-X}}[X]\leq\mu\}

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F},\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\leq\mu\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)\>.

∎

Let
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)=\{G\in\mathcal{F}\mid\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\geq\mu\}
and define
\mathcal{F}^{-}(\mu)
similarly.

Lemma 39
.

For all
\mu\in[0,B]
,
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
is a nonempty compact convex set (for the weak convergence of measures).

Proof.

It is nonempty since the Dirac distribution at
B
belongs to the set.

The set
\mathcal{F}
is compact, hence a sequence of distributions in
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
admits a convergent subsequence. Suppose then that we have a convergent sequence
(F_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, converging to
F
, and let’s show that
F\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
. We can rewrite

\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)=\{G\in\mathcal{F}\mid\mathbb{E}_{G}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\mu\}\>.

This is useful since the function
x\mapsto\max\{x,B\}
is bounded from above and continuous. We can thus apply the Portmanteau theorem to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{F}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\limsup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{F_{n}}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\mu\>.

We conclude that
F\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
, which is then compact.

To prove convexity, let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
and let
\alpha\in[0,1]
:
\alpha F+(1-\alpha)G\in\mathcal{F}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha F+(1-\alpha)G}[X]=\alpha\mathbb{E}_{F}[X]+(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\geq\alpha\mu+(1-\alpha)\mu=\mu\>.

∎

Lemma 40
.

\mu\mapsto\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
is an upper hemicontinuous correspondence.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{F}^{+}
is a compact-valued correspondence (Lemma
39
), it suffices to show that for all sequences
(\mu_{n})
and
(Q_{n})
with
Q_{n}\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu_{n})
, if
\mu_{n}\to\mu
then there exists a convergent subsequence of
(Q_{n})
, which converges to
Q\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)

[
40
, Proposition 9.8, p. 231]
. The existence of a convergent subsequence comes from the compactness of
\mathcal{F}
. The limit
Q
then belongs to
\mathcal{F}
since
\mathcal{F}
is closed. We need to show that
Q
belongs to
\{G\mid\mathbb{E}_{G}[X]\geq\mu\}
.

We can rewrite
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)=\{G\in\mathcal{F}\mid\mathbb{E}_{G}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\mu\}
and prove that
Q
belongs to
\{G\mid\mathbb{E}_{G}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\mu\}
.
This is useful since the function
x\mapsto\max\{x,B\}
is bounded from above and continuous. We can thus apply the Portmanteau theorem to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Q}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\limsup_{n}\mathbb{E}_{Q_{n}}[\max\{X,B\}]\geq\limsup_{n}\mu_{n}=\mu\>.

We conclude that
Q\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
. We have proved upper hemicontinuity.
∎

Lemma 41
.

The infimum in the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
is attained at a distribution in
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
.

Proof.

G\mapsto\mathrm{KL}(F,G)
is lower semicontinuous wrt the topology of weak convergence of measures and the set
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
over which the minimization is performed is compact, hence the functions attains its infimum at a point in
\mathcal{F}^{+}(\mu)
.
∎

F.1
Duality

For
(\lambda,F,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]
, let
H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)=\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]
, where we define
\log(x)=-\infty
for
x\leq 0
. Let
H^{-}(\lambda,F,u)=\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1+\lambda(X-u))]
.

Theorem 3
.

For all
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
u\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)\>,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,u)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,u^{-1}]}H^{-}(\lambda,F,u)\>.

Proof.

A proof of this statement for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
can be found in any one of
[
18
,
17
]
. The result for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
then follows from Lemma
38
.
∎

Lemma 42

(
[
18
]
, Lemma 14)
.

For all
(F,u)\in\mathcal{F}\times[0,B)
,
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)\leq-\log\left(1-\frac{u}{B}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
(F,u)\in\mathcal{F}\times[0,B)
. The proof relies on Theorem
3
and
X\geq 0
for all
X\in\mathrm{supp}(F)
. Using that
\log
is increasing on
(0,+\infty)
and
\mathbb{E}_{F}[1]=1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)\leq\sup_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}\log\left(1+\lambda u\right)=-\log\left(1-\frac{u}{B}\right)\>.

∎

F.2
Continuity and differentiability

Lemma 43
.

The function
\lambda,F,u\mapsto H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
is upper semicontinuous (jointly in all arguments) on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]
.

Proof.

Let
(\lambda_{n},F_{n},u_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]
be a sequence converging to
(\lambda,F,u)
. We want to prove that

\displaystyle\limsup\mathbb{E}_{F_{n}}[\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X-u_{n}))]\leq\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]\>.

By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, there exists real random variables
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},X
defined on a common probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
such that the law of
X_{n}
is
F_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the law of
X
is
F
and
(X_{n})
converges to
X
almost surely (hence also in probability).

The family
(X_{n})
has compact support, hence it is uniformly integrable.
By Vitali’s theorem, since
X_{n}
is uniformly integrable and converges in probability, it also converges in
L^{1}
.

We get that
\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n})\xrightarrow{a.s.}\lambda(X-u)
and
\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n})]\to\mathbb{E}[\lambda(X-u)]
. Now we want to translate this into a statement about the log.

Since
-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n})-\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n}))\geq 0
, by Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[-\lambda(X-u)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\limsup\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf\left(-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n})-\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n}))\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\liminf\left(\mathbb{E}\left[-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n})-\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n}))\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[-\lambda(X-u)\right]-\limsup\mathbb{E}\left[\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X_{n}-u_{n}))\right]\>.

Canceling the first term, we get the inequality we were after.
∎

Theorem 4
.

The function
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
) is continuous on
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B)
(resp.
\mathcal{F}\times(0,B]
).

Proof.

We follow the proof method of
[
5
]
(which applied to a slightly different setting).

We first prove lower semicontinuity. We want to apply Berge’s Maximum Theorem
[
9
, Theorem 2, p. 116]
to the correspondence
C(F,u)=\mathcal{F}^{+}(u)
and the function
f((F,u),G)=-\mathrm{KL}(F,G)
. We will obtain the upper semicontinuity of
f^{*}(F,u):=\inf\{f((F,u),G)\mid G\in C(F,u)\}=-\inf\{\mathrm{KL}(F,G)\mid G\in\mathcal{F}^{+}(u)\}
, which gives us the lower semicontinuity we are after. We need to show that

•

F,u\mapsto C(F,u)=\mathcal{F}^{+}(u)
is upper hemicontinuous: this is proved in Lemma
40
,

•

F,u,G\mapsto f(F,u,G)=-\mathrm{KL}(F,G)
is upper semicontinuous (jointly in all arguments): this is true since
\mathrm{KL}
is jointly lower semicontinuous
[
34
]
.

We have lower semicontinuity on
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]
.

To prove upper semicontinuity, we first use duality (Theorem
3
) to write
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
. Since we want to prove semicontinuity on
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B)
, we can take any
\varepsilon>0
and prove it on
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B-\varepsilon]
.

We want to apply Berge’s Maximum Theorem
[
9
, Theorem 2, p. 116]
to the correspondence
C(F,u)=\mathcal{[}0,(B-u)^{-1}]
and the function
f((F,u),\lambda)=\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]
. We will obtain the upper semicontinuity of
f^{*}(F,u)=\sup\{f((F,u),G)\mid G\in C(F,u)\}
, which is exactly what we are after. We need to show that

•

F,u\mapsto C(F,u)=[0,(B-u)^{-1}]
is upper hemicontinuous, nonempty and compact,

•

F,u,\lambda\mapsto f(F,u,\lambda)=\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]
is upper semicontinuous (jointly in all arguments): this is true by Lemma
43
.

For the first point, nonempty compact values are obvious for
u\leq B-\varepsilon
. The upper hemicontinuity comes from the continuity of the upper bound of the interval.
∎

Lemma 44
.

\lambda\mapsto H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
is strictly concave on
[0,(B-u)^{-1})
.

Proof.

For
\alpha\in(0,1)
, by strict concavity of the logarithm,

\displaystyle H^{+}(\alpha\lambda+(1-\alpha)\eta,F,u)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(\alpha(1-\lambda(X-u))+(1-\alpha)(1-\eta(X-u)))]

\displaystyle>\mathbb{E}_{F}[\alpha\log(1-\lambda(X-u))+(1-\alpha)\log(1-\eta(X-u))]

\displaystyle=\alpha H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)+(1-\alpha)H^{+}(\eta,F,u)\>.

The restriction to the interval
[0,(B-u)^{-1})
guarantees that all quantities appearing in logarithms above are finite.
∎

Lemma 45
.

(\lambda,u)\mapsto H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
is continuous on
\{(\lambda,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,B]\mid\lambda<(B-u)^{-1}\}
.

Proof.

We already have upper semicontinuity by Lemma
43
. We only need lower semicontinuity. That is, we need that for
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
and
u_{n}\to u
in that set, we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X-u_{n}))]\geq\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]\>.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\lambda\leq(B-u)^{-1}(1-\varepsilon)
. Then for
n
big enough,
\lambda_{n}\leq(B-u_{n})^{-1}(1-\varepsilon/2)
.
For all
n
large enough, we get
\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X-u_{n}))-\log(\varepsilon/2)\geq 0
. By Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle\liminf_{n}\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda_{n}(X-u_{n}))-\log(\varepsilon/2)]\geq\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))-\log(\varepsilon/2)]\>.

We cancel the
\log(\varepsilon/2)
term and get the lower semicontinuity.
∎

Let
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}\mathbb{E}_{F}[\log(1-\lambda(X-u))]=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
and
\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F,u)=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,u^{-1}]}H^{-}(\lambda,F,u)
.

Lemma 46
.

u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is continuous over the set
\{u\in[0,B)\mid\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)<(B-u)^{-1}\}
.

Proof.

We first show that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,B^{-1}]
, the function
u\mapsto\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
is continuous on
[0,B)
and the argmax is unique. This is not exactly continuity of
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
because of the
[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]
interval instead of
[0,(B-u)^{-1}]
.

We will apply Berge’s Maximum theorem
[
9
, page 116]
. For
\varepsilon\in(0,B^{-1}]
, let

\displaystyle\varphi(\lambda,u)

\displaystyle=H(\lambda,F,u)\>,

\displaystyle\Gamma(u)

\displaystyle=[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]\>,

\displaystyle M(u)

\displaystyle=\max\{H(\lambda,u)\mid\lambda\in\Gamma(u)\}\>,

\displaystyle\Phi(u)

\displaystyle=\argmax\{\varphi(\lambda,u)\mid\lambda\in\Gamma(u)\}\>.

We verify the hypotheses of the theorem for any
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
and
u<B-\varepsilon^{\prime}
:

•

H
is continuous on
\{(\lambda,u)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times[0,B]\mid\lambda<(B-u)^{-1}\}
, by Lemma
45
, which is a domain containing
\Gamma(u)\times\{u\}
for all
u
.

•

\Gamma
is nonempty (since
(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon\geq 0
), compact-valued (since
u\leq B-\varepsilon^{\prime}
) and continuous.

We obtain that
M
is continuous on
[0,B)
and that
\Phi
is upper hemicontinuous.

Now since
\varphi
is a strictly concave function of
\lambda
(by Lemma
44
) and
\Gamma
is convex, we can argue as in
[
40
, Theorem 9.17]
to prove that
\Phi
is a single-valued upper hemicontinuous correspondence, hence a continuous function.

Now that we have proved the continuity of the argmax restricted to the interval
[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]
, let’s prove the continuity of
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
over the set
\{u\in[0,B)\mid\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)<(B-u)^{-1}\}
.

let
u\in[0,B)
such that
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)<(B-u)^{-1}
. Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)\>,

\displaystyle\text{and }\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)

\displaystyle\leq(B-u)^{-1}-3\varepsilon\>.

Remark that by concavity of
H
in
\lambda
, for all
u
, if
\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)\neq(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon
then
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-u)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,u)
.

For
v
in some neighborhood of
u
, we have both
(B-v)^{-1}-\varepsilon>(B-u)^{-1}-2\varepsilon
and
\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-v)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,v)<(B-u)^{-1}-2\varepsilon
. This means that for all
v
in that neighborhood,
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,v)=\argmax_{\lambda\in[0,(B-v)^{-1}-\varepsilon]}H^{+}(\lambda,F,v)
. The continuity of the
\varepsilon
version then gives continuity of
\lambda_{\star}^{+}
at
u
.
∎

Lemma 47

(Theorem 6 in
[
18
]
)
.

For all
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
u\in(m(F),B]
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is differentiable and

\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)}{\partial u}=\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)\>.

(37)

For all
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
u\in[0,m(F))
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,u)
is differentiable and

\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,u)}{\partial u}=-\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F,u)\>.

(38)

F.3
Convexity

Lemma 48
.

The functions
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
and
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
are jointly convex on
\mathcal{F}\times[0,B]
.

Proof.

We prove the result for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
, but the proof for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
is identical.
Let
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
,
u_{1},u_{2}\in[0,B]
and let
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
be distributions at which the infimum is attained in
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{1},u_{1})
and
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{2},u_{2})
respectively (which exist by Lemma
41
). For all
\alpha\in[0,1]
,
\alpha G_{1}+(1-\alpha)G_{2}
has expectation
\alpha u_{1}+(1-\alpha)u_{2}
. Hence for all
\alpha\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\alpha F_{1}+(1-\alpha)F_{2},\alpha u_{1}+(1-\alpha)u_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{KL}(\alpha F_{1}+(1-\alpha)F_{2},\alpha G_{1}+(1-\alpha)G_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathrm{KL}(F_{1},G_{1})+(1-\alpha)\mathrm{KL}(F_{2},G_{2})

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{1},u_{1})+(1-\alpha)\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{2},u_{2})

The first inequality follows from the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
as an infimum and the second inequality comes from the joint convexity of the Kullback-Leibler divergence. We have proved joint convexity.
∎

Lemma 49

(Theorem 5 in
[
18
]
)
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
u^{+}(F)=B-\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{B-X}\right]}\geq m(F)
. We have

\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=0\>\iff\>u\leq m(F)\>,

(39)

u\in(m(F),u^{+}(F)]\>\implies\>\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(X-u)}\right]=1\>,

(40)

and

\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\frac{1}{B-u}\>\iff\>u\geq u^{+}(F)\>.

(41)

Proof.

First, we have

\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=0\>\iff\>u\leq m(F)\>.

If
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=0
, then
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)=H^{+}(0,F,u)=0
, hence
u\leq m(F)
. The other direction is obtained in Theorem 5 in
[
18
]
.

Let
u^{+}(F)=B-\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{B-X}\right]}
and
u^{-}(F)=\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{X}\right]}
. Then,

\displaystyle u\in(m(F),u^{+}(F)]\>\implies\>\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(X-u)}\right]=1\>,

Moreover,

\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\frac{1}{B-u}\>\iff\>u\geq u^{+}(F)\>.

If
u\geq u^{+}(F)
, then
\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{B-u}{B-X}\right]\leq 1
, hence
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\frac{1}{B-u}
by Theorem 5 in
[
18
]
. Assume that there exists
u<u^{+}(F)
, such that
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\frac{1}{B-u}
. Then, by equation (
40
), we obtain that
1=\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{1-\frac{X-u}{B-u}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{B-u}{B-X}\right]
. This condition can be rewritten as
u=u(F)
, hence contradicting
u<u^{+}(F)
. Therefore, we have shown
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=\frac{1}{B-u}
implies that
u\geq u^{+}(F)
.
∎

Lemma 50
.

The function
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is strictly convex on
(m(F),B]
. The function
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,u)
is strictly convex on
[0,m(F))
.

Proof.

We prove the result for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
. The proof for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
is similar.

Using Lemma
47
,
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is strictly convex for
u>m(F)
if and only if
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing for
u>m(F)
.

Using (
39
),
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is null for
u\leq m(F)
. Therefore,
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{\pm}(F,u)
is not strictly convex on those intervals.

Using (
41
), we obtain directly that
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing for
u\geq u^{+}(F)
.

Suppose towards contradiction that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is not increasing for
(m(F),u^{+}(F))
. Therefore, there exists an open
\mathcal{O}\subseteq(m(F),u^{+}(F))
, such that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is constant on
\mathcal{O}
, i.e. there exists
c_{\mathcal{O}}\in\left[0,\frac{1}{B-\inf_{u\in\mathcal{O}}u}\right]
such that
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)=c_{\mathcal{O}}
. Using (
39
-
41
), we know that
c_{\mathcal{O}}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{B-\inf_{u\in\mathcal{O}}u}\right)
. On
\mathcal{O}
,
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is constant, hence it is continuously differentiable with null derivative. Since
\mathcal{O}\subseteq(m(F),u^{+}(F))
, (
40
) defines implicitly
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
as satisfying

\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(X-u)}\right]=1\>.

Since
\mathcal{O}\subseteq(m(F),u^{+}(F))
, we have
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)\in\left(0,\frac{1}{B-\inf_{u\in\mathcal{O}}u}\right)
. Therefore, the function
(u,x)\mapsto\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(x-u)}
is bounded on
[0,B]\times\mathcal{O}
, hence integrable, and the function
u\mapsto\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(x-u)}
is continuously differentiable. Moreover, the function
x\mapsto\frac{1}{\left(1-\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)(x-u)\right)^{2}}
is strictly positive and bounded on
[0,B]
, hence integrable with strictly positive integrable. Having checked all the conditions to interchange the derivative with the expectation, differentiating the above yields

\displaystyle 0=\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[-\frac{\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)+(u-X)\frac{\partial\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)}{\partial u}}{\left(1-(X-u)\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)\right)^{2}}\right]=-c_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{\left(1-(X-u)c_{\mathcal{O}}\right)^{2}}\right]<0\>,

where the strict inequality is obtained since we show that
c_{\mathcal{O}}>0
and
\mathbb{E}_{F}\left[\frac{1}{\left(1-(X-u)c_{\mathcal{O}}\right)^{2}}\right]>0
. This is a contradiction, hence such
\mathcal{O}\subset(m(F),u^{+}(F))
doesn’t exist. Therefore,
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing on
(m(F),u^{+}(F))
.

Since the convexity already gave that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing on
(m(F),B]
. The fact that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing on
(m(F),u^{+}(F))
and on
[u^{+}(F),B]
, yields that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing on
(m(F),B]
.
∎

Lemma 51
.

u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is equal to zero on
[0,m(F)]
and increasing on
(m(F),B]
.

Proof.

We already proved that
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is equal to zero on
[0,m(F)]
. Since
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,m(F))=0
,
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
is nonnegative and strictly convex for
u>m(F)
, then
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,u)
is increasing on
(m(F),B]
.
∎

Lemma 52
.

For all
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
with means
m(F)\leq m(G)
and for all
w\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

•

If
\max\{w_{1},w_{2}\}>0
, then
\mu\mapsto w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)
is strictly convex on
[m(F),m(G)]
.

•

If
\min\{w_{1},w_{2}\}>0
, then
\mu\mapsto w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)
is strictly convex on
[0,B]
.

Proof.

For
\mu\leq m(F)
, the function is equal to
w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)
, which is strictly convex unless
w_{2}=0
. For
\mu\geq m(G)
, the function is equal to
w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)
, which is strictly convex unless
w_{1}=0
. In the interval
(m(F),m(G))
, it is the sum of two convex functions, one of which is strictly convex. Furthermore, the function is continuous at
m(F)
and
m(G)
.
∎

F.3.1
More continuity, using convexity

Lemma 53
.

Let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
with means
m(F)\leq m(G)
in
(0,B)
and
w\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))=\inf_{\mu\in[m(F),m(G)]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))\>.

Proof.

On
[0,m(F)]
,
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)
is constant equal to 0 and
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)
is non-increasing, hence the minimum over that interval is attained at
m(F)
. We argue similarly for the interval
[m(G),B]
.
∎

Lemma 54
.

Let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
with means in
(0,B)
and
w\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
. Then

1.

(F,G,w)\mapsto\inf_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

2.

If
\max\{w_{1},w_{2}\}>0
,
\mu_{\star}(F,G,w)=\argmin_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))
is unique and continuous on
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

Proof.

We can restrict the inf to
[\mu_{F},\mu_{G}]
by Lemma
53
.

We will apply Berge’s Maximum theorem
[
9
, page 116]
. Let

\displaystyle\varphi(\mu,F,G,w)

\displaystyle=-w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)-w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)\>,

\displaystyle\Gamma(F,G,w)

\displaystyle=[\mu_{F},\mu_{G}]\>,

\displaystyle M(F,G,w)

\displaystyle=\max\{\varphi(\mu,F,G,w)\mid\mu\in\Gamma(F,G,w)\}\>,

\displaystyle\Phi(F,G,w)

\displaystyle=\argmax\{\varphi(\mu,F,G,w)\mid\mu\in\Gamma(F,G,w)\}\>.

We verify the hypotheses of the theorem:

•

\varphi
is continuous on
[\mu_{F},\mu_{G}]\times\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\times C
, by Theorem
4
since
\mu_{F},\mu_{G}\in(0,B)
.

•

\Gamma
is nonempty, compact-valued and continuous (since constant).

We obtain that
M
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
and that
\Phi
is upper hemicontinuous.

Now since
\varphi
is a strictly concave function of
\mu
(by Lemma
52
) and
\Gamma
is convex, we can argue as in
[
40
, Theorem 9.17]
to prove that
\Phi
is a single-valued upper hemicontinuous correspondence, hence a continuous function.
∎

Lemma 55
.

Let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
with means
m(F)<m(G)
in
(0,B)
and
w\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
such that
\min\{w_{1},w_{2}\}>0
.
The value
\mu_{\star}(F,G,w)=\argmin_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))
(unique by Lemma
54
) belongs to the interval
(m(F),m(G))
and is such that both
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}
are positive.

Proof.

We know by Lemma
53
that the minimum is attained inside
[m(F),m(G)]
. We only need to exclude the boundaries.

The function
\mu\mapsto w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)
is differentiable on
(m(F),m(G))
with derivative
w_{1}\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,\mu)-w_{2}\lambda_{\star}^{-}(G,\mu)
. If we show that this derivatives takes the value zero in the open interval, then we prove the result.

For
\mu>m(F)
in a neighborhood of
m(F)
,
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F,\mu)
tends to 0 by continuity (Lemma
46
) and
\lambda_{\star}^{-}(G,\mu)>\lambda_{\star}^{-}(G,\frac{m(F)+m(G)}{2})>0
. We get that close to
m(F)
, the derivative is negative. Similarly, we get that the derivative is positive close to
m(G)
. We conclude that the infimum is indeed attained inside the open interval.
∎

Lemma 56
.

Let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
with means
m(F)<m(G)
in
(0,B)
and
w_{1}>0
. Then
w_{2}\mapsto\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{2}^{\prime}>w_{2}\geq 0
. Then by Lemma
55
, since
w_{2}^{\prime}>0
, there exists
\mu^{\prime}\in[0,B]
with
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu^{\prime})>0
such that
\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))=w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu^{\prime})+w_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu^{\prime})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))

\displaystyle=w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu^{\prime})+w_{2}^{\prime}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu^{\prime})

\displaystyle>w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu^{\prime})+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))\>.

∎

Lemma 57
.

Let
F,G,F_{1},\ldots,F_{K}\in\mathcal{F}
with means in
(0,B)
.

The function
w\mapsto\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)+w_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu))
is concave on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.

The function
w\mapsto\min_{j\neq 1}\min_{\mu\in[0,B]}(w_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{1},\mu)+w_{j}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{j},\mu))
is concave on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}
.

Proof.

These functions are minimums of linear functions, hence concave.
∎

F.4
Properties of the characteristic time

Let
\bm{F}\in\mathcal{F}^{K}
and
i^{\star}=i^{\star}(\bm{F})
, supposed unique.
Recall that

\displaystyle T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{w\in\triangle_{K}}\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}\>,

\displaystyle w^{\star}(\bm{F})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{w\in\triangle_{K}}\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}\>,

\displaystyle T_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\triangle_{K}\\
w_{i^{\star}}=\beta\end{subarray}}\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}\>,

\displaystyle w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\triangle_{K}\\
w_{i^{\star}}=\beta\end{subarray}}\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}\>.

Lemma 58
.

The functions
{T^{\star}}^{-1}
and
{T^{\star}_{\beta}}^{-1}
are continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}
. The correspondences
w^{\star}
and
w^{\star}_{\beta}
are upper hemicontinuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}
with compact convex values.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i}=\left\{\bm{F}\in\mathcal{F}^{K}\mid i\in i^{\star}(\bm{F})\right\}
. Since
\bigcup_{i\in[K]}\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i}=\mathcal{F}^{K}
, it is enough to show the property for all
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i}
for
i\in[K]
. Let
i^{\star}\in[K]
.

First, the function
(w,\bm{F})\mapsto\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i^{\star}},u)\right\}
is continuous on
\triangle_{K}\times\mathcal{F}^{K}
by Lemma
54
and the fact that a minimum of continuous functions is continuous. It is concave in
w
by Lemma
57
.

The correspondence
(w,\bm{F})\mapsto\triangle_{K}
is nonempty compact-valued and continuous (since constant). By Berge’s maximum theorem, we get that
T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}
and that
w^{\star}(\bm{F})
is upper hemicontinuous with compact values. By
[
40
, Theorem 9.17]
, the concavity of the function being maximized implies that
w^{\star}(\bm{F})
is convex-valued.

The correspondence
(w,\bm{F})\mapsto\triangle_{K}\cap\{w_{i^{\star}}=\beta\}
is nonempty compact-valued and continuous (since constant). By Berge’s maximum theorem, we get that
T_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}
is continuous on
\mathcal{F}^{K}_{i^{\star}}
and that
w^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})
is upper hemicontinuous with compact values. By
[
40
, Theorem 9.17]
, the concavity of the function being maximized implies that
w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})
is convex-valued.
∎

Lemma 59
.

If
i^{\star}(\bm{F})
is a singleton and
\beta\in(0,1)
, then
T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}>0
and
T_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}>0
.

Proof.

\displaystyle T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sup_{w\in\triangle_{K}}\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+\frac{1}{K}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}>0\>,

since we proved that the inner infimum is positive for nonzero coefficients and
\mu_{i}<\mu_{i^{\star}}
. The proof for
T_{\beta}^{\star}
is similar.
∎

Lemma 60
.

If
i^{\star}(\bm{F})
is a singleton and
\beta\in(0,1)
, then for all
i\in[K]
,
w^{\star}_{i}(\bm{F})>0
and
w^{\star}_{\beta,i}(\bm{F})>0
.

Proof.

We proceed by contradiction. If
i^{\star}(\bm{F})
is unique and there exists
j
with
w^{\star}_{j}(\bm{F})=0
, then we show
T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}=0
, which is absurd by Lemma
59
. If
j=i^{\star}
we have

\displaystyle T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}=\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}w_{i}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\leq\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}w_{i}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},F_{i})=0\>.

If
j\neq i^{\star}
,

\displaystyle T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\min_{i\neq i^{\star}}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{j}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{j},u)\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in[0,B]}w_{i^{\star}}^{\star}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)=0\>.

A similar proof holds for
T_{\beta}^{\star}
.
∎

Lemma 61
.

If
i^{\star}(\bm{F})
is a singleton and
\beta\in(0,1)
, then

•

for all
i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})
,
\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{w_{i^{\star}}^{\star}(\bm{F})\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{i}^{\star}(\bm{F})\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}=T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}
,

•

for all
i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})
,
\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\beta\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+w_{\beta,i}^{\star}(\bm{F})\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}=T_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}
,

•

w^{\star}(\bm{F})
and
w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})
are singletons: the optimal allocations are unique.

Proof.

At the optimal allocations, all
w_{i}
are positive. Suppose w.l.o.g. that 1 is the best arm. By dividing by
w_{1}
and defining

G_{j}(x)=\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{j},u)\right)\>,

we obtain directly that

\displaystyle T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}=\max_{w\in\triangle_{K},w_{1}>0}w_{1}\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(\frac{w_{j}}{w_{1}}\right)\>.

Let
w^{\star}\in w^{\star}(\bm{F})
. Then, using the above result, we obtain

\displaystyle w^{\star}\in\argmax_{w\in\triangle_{K}}w_{1}\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(\frac{w_{j}}{w_{1}}\right)

Introducing
x_{j}^{\star}=\frac{w^{\star}_{j}}{w_{1}^{\star}}
for all
j\neq 1
, using that
\sum_{j\in[K]}w_{j}^{\star}=1
, one has

\displaystyle w_{1}^{\star}=\frac{1}{1+\sum_{j=2}^{K}x_{j}^{\star}}\quad\text{ and, for }j\geq 2,w^{\star}_{j}=\frac{x_{j}^{\star}}{1+\sum_{j=2}^{K}x_{j}^{\star}}\>.

If
x^{\star}
is unique, then so is
w^{\star}
.

Since it is optimal,
\{x_{j}^{\star}\}_{j=2}^{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}
belongs to

\argmax_{\{x_{j}\}_{j=2}^{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{K-1}}\frac{\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(x_{j}\right)}{1+\sum_{j=2}^{K}x_{j}}

(42)

Let’s show that all the
G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}\right)
have to be equal. Let
\mathcal{O}=\left\{i\in[K]\setminus\{1\}\mid G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}\right)=\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}\right)\right\}
and
\mathcal{A}=[K]\setminus(\{1\}\cup\mathcal{O})
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}\neq\emptyset
. For all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
b\in\mathcal{O}
, one has
G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}\right)>G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}\right)
. Using the continuity of the
G_{j}
functions and the fact that they are increasing (Lemma
56
), there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall j\in\mathcal{A},i\in\mathcal{O},\quad G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}-\varepsilon/|\mathcal{A}|\right)>G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}+\varepsilon/|\mathcal{O}|\right)>G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}\right)\>.

We introduce
\bar{x}_{j}=x_{j}^{\star}-\varepsilon/|\mathcal{A}|
for all
j\in\mathcal{A}
and
\bar{x}_{i}=x_{i}^{\star}+\varepsilon/|\mathcal{O}|
for all
i\in\mathcal{O}
, hence
\sum_{j=2}^{K}\bar{x}_{j}=\sum_{j=2}^{K}x_{j}^{\star}
. There exists
i\in\mathcal{O}
such that
\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(\bar{x}_{j}\right)=G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}+\varepsilon/|\mathcal{O}|\right)
, hence

\displaystyle\frac{\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(\bar{x}_{j}\right)}{1+\bar{x}_{2}+\ldots\bar{x}_{K}}=\frac{G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}+\varepsilon/|\mathcal{O}|\right)}{1+x_{2}^{\star}+\cdots+x_{K}^{\star}}>\frac{G_{i}\left(x_{i}^{\star}\right)}{1+x_{2}^{\star}+\cdots+x_{K}^{\star}}=\frac{\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}\right)}{1+x_{2}^{\star}+\cdots+x_{K}^{\star}}\>.

This is a contradiction with the fact that
x^{\star}
belongs to (
42
). Therefore, we have
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
.

We have proved that there is a unique value by
y^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, such that for all
j\neq 1
,
G_{j}\left(x_{j}^{\star}\right)=y^{\star}
. Now since
G_{j}
is increasing, this defines a unique value for
x_{j}^{\star}
, equal to
G_{j}^{-1}(y^{\star})
.

For
y
in the intersection of the ranges of all
G_{j}
, let
x_{j}(y)=G_{j}^{-1}(y)
.
y^{\star}
belongs to

\argmax_{y\in\left[0,\min_{j\neq 1}\lim_{+\infty}G_{j}(x)\right)}\frac{y}{1+\sum_{j\neq 1}x_{j}(y)}\>.

(43)

For
\beta\in(0,1)
, the same results (and proof) hold for
w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})
by noting that

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})^{-1}=\max_{w\in\triangle_{K}:w_{1}=\beta}\beta\min_{j\neq 1}G_{j}\left(\frac{w_{j}}{\beta}\right)\>.

Let
w^{\beta}\in w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})
, since we have equality at the equilibrium, we obtain for all
j\neq 1
,

\displaystyle\beta G_{j}\left(\frac{w^{\beta}_{j}}{\beta}\right)=T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})^{-1}\>,

Using the inverse mapping
x_{j}
, we obtain for all
j\neq 1
,

\displaystyle w^{\beta}_{j}=\beta x_{j}\left(\frac{1}{T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})\beta}\right)\>.

Therefore, we have shown that
w_{\beta}^{\star}(\bm{F})=\{w^{\beta}\}
, where

\displaystyle w^{\beta}_{i}=\begin{cases}\beta x_{i}\left(\frac{1}{T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})\beta}\right)&\text{if }i\neq i^{\star}\\
\beta&\text{else}\end{cases}\>.

∎

Lemma 62
.

T^{\star}_{1/2}(\bm{F})\leq 2T^{\star}(\bm{F})
and with
\beta^{\star}=w^{\star}_{i^{\star}}(\bm{F})
,

\displaystyle\frac{T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}}{T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})^{-1}}\leq\max\left\{\frac{\beta^{\star}}{\beta},\frac{1-\beta^{\star}}{1-\beta}\right\}\>.

Proof.

Define for each non-negative vector
\bm{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K}
,

\displaystyle f(\bm{\psi}):=\min_{i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})}\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left\{\psi_{i^{\star}}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+\psi_{i}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right\}\>.

T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}
is the maximum of
f(\bm{\psi})
over probability vectors
\bm{\psi}
. Here, we instead define
f
for all non-negative vectors, and proceed by varying the total budget of measurement effort available
\sum_{a\in[K]}\psi_{a}
.

f
is non-decreasing in
\psi_{i}
for all
i
.
f
is homogeneous of degree
1
. That is
f(c\bm{\psi})=cf(\bm{\psi})
for all
c\geq 1
. For each
c_{1}
,
c_{2}>0
define

g\left(c_{1},c_{2}\right)=\max\left\{f(\bm{\psi})\mid\bm{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K},\>\psi_{i^{\star}(\bm{F})}=c_{1},\sum_{i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})}\psi_{i}\leq c_{2},\right\}

The function
g
inherits key properties of
f
; it is also non-decreasing and homogeneous of degree 1 . We have

\displaystyle T^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{f(\bm{\psi})\mid\bm{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K},\>\psi_{i^{\star}(\bm{F})}=\beta,\sum_{i\in[K]}\psi_{i}=1\right\}

\displaystyle=\max\left\{f(\bm{\psi})\mid\bm{\psi}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{K},\>\psi_{i^{\star}(\bm{F})}=\beta,\sum_{i\neq i^{\star}(\bm{F})}\psi_{i}\leq 1-\beta\right\}

\displaystyle=g(\beta,1-\beta)

where the second equality uses that
f
is non-decreasing. Similarly,
T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}=g\left(\beta^{\star},1-\beta^{\star}\right)
where
\beta^{\star}=w^{\star}_{i^{\star}(\bm{F})}(\bm{F})
. Setting

r:=\max\left\{\frac{\beta^{\star}}{\beta},\frac{1-\beta^{\star}}{1-\beta}\right\}

implies
r\beta\geq\beta^{\star}
and
r(1-\beta)\geq 1-\beta^{\star}
. Therefore

rT^{\star}_{\beta}(\bm{F})^{-1}=rg(\beta,1-\beta)=g(r\beta,r(1-\beta))\geq g\left(\beta^{\star},1-\beta^{\star}\right)=T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}\>.

Taking
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
, yields that
T^{\star}(\bm{F})^{-1}\leq 2\max\{\beta^{\star},1-\beta^{\star}\}T^{\star}_{1/2}(\bm{F})^{-1}\leq 2T^{\star}_{1/2}(\bm{F})^{-1}
.
∎

Lemma 63
.

Let
G_{i}(x)=\inf_{u\in[0,B]}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right)
for
x\in[0,+\infty)
and
i\neq i^{\star}
. Then,

\lim_{x\to+\infty}G_{i}(x)=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},m(F_{i}))\>.

Proof.

Let
u_{i}(x)\in\argmin_{u\in[0,B]}\left(\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)+x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u)\right)
.
It is easy to see that
G_{i}(0)=0
and
u_{i}(0)=m(F_{1})
.
Likewise, we have
u_{i}(x)=_{+\infty}m(F_{i})+o(1)
by considering
(w_{i^{\star}},w_{i})
instead of
x_{i}=\frac{w_{i}}{w_{i^{\star}}}
.
By continuity of
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u)
and using the definition of
u_{i}(x)

\lim_{x\to+\infty}G_{i}(x)=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},m(F_{i}))+\lim_{x\to+\infty}x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))\>.

Using the deviations bounds on the
u\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,u)
(e.g. Lemma 6 in
[
19
]
), we obtain that

0<x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))\leq x\frac{(m(F_{i})-u_{i}(x))^{2}}{2}\>.

Therefore, a sufficient condition to obtain
\lim_{x\to+\infty}x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))=0
is to show that
u_{i}(x)=_{+\infty}m(F_{i})+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\right)
. The first order condition of optimality on
u_{i}(x)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle x\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))}{\partial u}=-\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u_{i}(x))}{\partial u}\iff\quad

\displaystyle x\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))=\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u_{i}(x))\>,

where we used Lemma
47
for the equivalent formulation.

Using that
u\mapsto\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F,u)
is decreasing for
u<m(F_{i^{\star}})
(Lemma
50
and Lemma
47
) yields

\displaystyle x\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))=\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},u_{i}(x))\leq\lambda_{\star}^{-}(F_{i^{\star}},m(F_{i}))\leq\frac{1}{m(F_{i})}\>.

Using that
\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F_{i},u)\geq\frac{u-m(F_{i})}{u(B-u)}
(Lemma 12 in
[
18
]
) and denoting
y(x)=u_{i}(x)-m(F_{i})=_{+\infty}o(1)
, we obtain

\frac{1}{m(F_{i})}\geq x\lambda_{\star}^{+}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))\geq\frac{xy(x)}{(m(F_{i})+y(x))(B-m(F_{i})-y(x))}\>.

Suppose towards contradiction that
y(x)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{x})
doesn’t hold, i.e.
\lim_{+\infty}xy(x)=+\infty
. Using that
y(x)=_{+\infty}o(1)
and taking the limit in the above inequality yields

\frac{1}{m(F_{i})}\geq\frac{\lim_{+\infty}xy(x)}{m(F_{i})(B-m(F_{i}))}=+\infty\>,

which is a direct contradiction. Therefore, we have shown that
y(x)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{x})
. We showed above that a sufficient condition to conclude was
y(x)=_{+\infty}o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}\right)
. Therefore, we have obtained that
\lim_{+\infty}x\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{i},u_{i}(x))=0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix G
Boundary crossing probability bounds

In order to analyze the algorithms presented in this paper, we need to quantify probabilities of the form
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2})
for
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
two independent real random variables. We first show how such bounds can be obtained by quantifying the individual deviations
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{i}\geq u)
and
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{i}\leq u)
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
(the so-called Boundary Crossing Probabilities). Then we prove upper and lower bounds on those probabilities when
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are obtained from a Dirichlet sampler.

G.1
From one arm to two

As remarked in Appendix
D.2.1
, studying BAI randomized algorithms require to control probability of the form
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2})
where
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are two independent real random variables.
Thanks to Lemma
64
, it is possible to obtain those by using Boundary Crossing Probability (BCP) bounds, which are extensively studied in the regret minimization literature.
Therefore, while it is based on simple calculations, Lemma
64
is a powerful result of independent interest.

Lemma 64
.

Let
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
be two independent real random variables with cdf
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
. Let
x\in\argmax_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq u)\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq u)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq x)\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq x)\leq\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq g\big(\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq x)\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq x)\big)\>.

where
g(u)=u(1-\log(u))
for all
u\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

To ease the notation, we introduce the cdfs
F_{1}(u)=\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq u)
and
F_{2}(u)=\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\leq u)
. We can suppose that there exists
u\in\mathbb{R}
with
(1-F_{2}(u))F_{1}(u)>0
. Otherwise the probability of
\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1}
is 0, and both bounds are 0 as well.
We start by proving the upper bound.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=\int_{u}\int_{v}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{u\leq x}\int_{v\leq x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)+\int_{u\leq x}\int_{v>x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{u>x}\int_{v\leq x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)+\int_{u>x}\int_{v>x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

The second of those four integrals is equal to zero. We now bound integrals 1, 3, and 4.

1.

For
x
such that
F_{2}(x)<1
,

\displaystyle\int_{u\leq x}\int_{v\leq x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{u\leq x}F_{1}(u)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{u\leq x}\frac{1}{1-F_{2}(u)}(1-F_{2}(u))F_{1}(u)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{u\leq x}(1-F_{2}(u))F_{1}(u)\right)\int_{u\leq x}\frac{1}{1-F_{2}(u)}dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=-\log(1-F_{2}(x))\sup_{u\leq x}(1-F_{2}(u))F_{1}(u)\>.

3.

\displaystyle\int_{u>x}\int_{v\leq x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=F_{1}(x)(1-F_{2}(x))\>.

4.

For
x
such that
F_{1}(x)>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{u>x}\int_{v>x}\mathds{1}\{u\geq v\}dF_{1}(v)dF_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\int_{v>x}(1-F_{2}(v))dF_{1}(v)

\displaystyle=\int_{v>x}F_{1}(v)(1-F_{2}(v))\frac{1}{F_{1}(v)}dF_{1}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sup_{v>x}F_{1}(v)(1-F_{2}(v))\right)\int_{v>x}\frac{1}{F_{1}(v)}dF_{1}(v)

\displaystyle=-\log(F_{1}(x))\sup_{v>x}F_{1}(v)(1-F_{2}(v))\>.

Putting things together:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq-\log(1-F_{2}(x))\sup_{u\leq x}(1-F_{2}(u))F_{1}(u)+F_{1}(x)(1-F_{2}(x))

\displaystyle\quad-\log(F_{1}(x))\sup_{v>x}F_{1}(v)(1-F_{2}(v))\>.

Taking for
x
the argmax over
\mathbb{R}
(which verifies
F_{1}(x)>0
and
F_{2}(x)<1
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq(1-F_{2}(x))F_{1}(x)\left[1-\log((1-F_{2}(x))F_{1}(x))\right]

We now prove the lower bound. For
x\in\mathbb{R}
, by independence of
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq x\geq\theta_{1})=\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq x)\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq x)=(1-F_{2}(x))F_{1}(x)\>.

∎

G.2
Upper bounds

Theorem
5
gives a tight upper bound on the BCP.

Theorem 5
.

Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in[0,B]^{n}
, let
\hat{F}_{n}
be the corresponding empirical distribution and let
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\hat{F}_{n},\mu)\right)\>,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\leq\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(\hat{F}_{n},\mu)\right)\>.

Proof.

We first prove the bound involving
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
. This proof is extracted from the proof of Lemma 15 of
[
36
]
.

Let
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}
be independent exponential random variables with parameter 1.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{R_{i}}{\sum_{j}R_{j}}X_{i}\geq\mu)=\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}(X_{i}-\mu)\geq 0)\>.

For
t\geq 0
, we can compose with exponentials and use Markov’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(\exp t\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}(X_{i}-\mu)\geq 1)\leq\mathbb{E}e^{t\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}(X_{i}-\mu)}\>.

By independence, this last expression is equal to
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}e^{t(X_{i}-\mu)R_{i}}
. By a simple computation (See
[
36
]
) we get, for
t\in[0,\frac{1}{X_{i}-\mu})
if
X_{i}\geq\mu
and for
t\geq 0
otherwise,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}e^{t(X_{i}-\mu)R_{i}}=\frac{1}{1-t(X_{i}-\mu)}\>.

We have proved that for all
t\in[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-n\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1-t(X_{i}-\mu))\right)\>.

This is then also true for
t
minimizing the right-hand side.

It remains to show that
\sup_{t\in[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu})}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1-t(X_{i}-\mu))=\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\hat{F}_{n},\mu)
.

From
[
18
]
, Theorem 8, for any distribution
F
with support in
[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)

\displaystyle=\sup_{t\in[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu}]}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim F}[\log(1-t(X-\mu))]\>.

Applying this to
\hat{F}_{n}
gives
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\hat{F}_{n},\mu)=\sup_{t\in[0,\frac{1}{B-\mu}]}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1-t(X_{i}-\mu))
. The only difference with our target is that the supremum is over the closed interval and not the right-open interval, but either the sup is the same by continuity if there is no
X_{i}
equal to
B
, or the value at
1/(B-\mu)
is
-\infty
and hence not equal to the sup.

We now prove the bound involving
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
. Let
\hat{F}_{n}^{B-X}
be the empirical distribution corresponding to
(B-X_{1},\ldots,B-X_{n})
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}X\leq\mu)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n})}(L^{\top}(B-X)\geq B-\mu)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\hat{F}_{n}^{B-X},B-\mu)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(\hat{F}_{n},\mu)\right)\>.

The last equality follows from Lemma
38
.
∎

Corollary 2
.

Let
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\in[0,B]^{n}
, and let
Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m})\in[0,B]^{m}
. let
\hat{F}_{n,X}
be empirical distribution corresponding to
X
(and define
\hat{F}_{m,Y}
similarly). Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L_{X}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n}),L_{Y}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{m})}(L_{X}^{\top}X\geq L_{Y}^{\top}Y)

\displaystyle\leq f\left(-\inf_{\mu\in[0,B]}\left(n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(\hat{F}_{n,X},\mu)+m\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(\hat{F}_{m,Y},\mu)\right)\right)\>.

where
f(x)=(1+x)e^{-x}
.

Proof.

Combine the two bounds of Theorem
5
using Lemma
64
.
∎

G.3
Lower bounds

Lemma
65
gives a first, coarse lower bound on the BCP under a Dirichlet sampler.
This result crucially relies on the fact that
\{0,B\}
have been added to the support.

Lemma 65
.

Let
X=(B,0,X_{1}\ldots,X_{n})\in[0,B]^{n+2}
and
u\in(0,B)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}X\geq u]

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{u}{B}\right)^{n+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}X\leq u]\geq\left(\frac{u}{B}\right)^{n+1}\>.

Proof.

We consider
\tilde{X}=(B,0,0\ldots,0)\in[0,B]^{n+2}
, use that the marginals of Dirichlet are Beta distributions and the Beta-Binomial trick (e.g.
[
2
]
) to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}X\geq u]\geq\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}\tilde{X}\geq u]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\mathrm{Beta}(1,n+1)}\left[w\geq\frac{u}{B}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{k\sim\mathrm{Bin}\left(n+1,\frac{u}{B}\right)}[k\leq 0]

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{u}{B}\right)^{n+1}\>.

Similarly, considering
\tilde{X}=(B,0,B\ldots,B)\in[0,B]^{n+2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}X\leq u]\geq\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1^{n+2})}[L^{\top}\tilde{X}\leq u]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{w\sim\mathrm{Beta}(1,n+1)}\left[w\geq 1-\frac{u}{B}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{k\sim\mathrm{Bin}\left(n+1,1-\frac{u}{B}\right)}[k\leq 0]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{u}{B}\right)^{n+1}\>.

∎

In the rest of this section, we derive a tighter lower bound on the BCP which leads to a tight lower bound on the probability that one Dirichlet sample exceeds another (Theorem
8
). These result rely on a discretization argument and on deriving lower bounds for multinomial distributions.

G.3.1
Multinomial distributions

Theorem
6
gives a tight lower bound on the BCP for multinomial distributions.

Theorem 6
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}\in[0,B]
with
X_{M}=B
and let
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{M}
with
\beta_{i}>0
for all
i
. Define
n=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}
. For all
\mu\in[0,B]
and
q\in\triangle_{M}
such that
q^{\top}X\geq\mu
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim Dir(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}\left(\frac{\beta}{n},q\right)\right)\>.

where
\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}(p,q)
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence between multinomial distributions with probability vectors
p
and
q
.

Proof.

The proof is strongly inspired by the works of
[
18
]
and
[
7
]
who use lower bound on the BCP for analyzing regret minimization algorithms.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{q}=\{p\in\triangle_{M}\mid\forall i\in[M-1],p_{i}\leq q_{i}\}
. For
p\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
, we necessarily have
p_{M}\geq q_{M}
and
p^{\top}X\geq q^{\top}X\geq\mu
. From that inequality we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)\geq\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L\in\mathcal{S}_{q})\>.

We now quantify that probability, using the pdf of a Dirichlet distribution,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L\in\mathcal{S}_{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\int_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{q}}\prod_{i=1}^{M}x_{i}^{\beta_{i}-1}dx

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}q_{M}^{\beta_{M}-1}\prod_{j=1}^{M-1}\int_{x_{j}=0}^{q_{j}}x_{j}^{\beta_{j}-1}dx_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}q_{M}^{\beta_{M}-1}\prod_{j=1}^{M-1}\frac{q_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\frac{\beta_{M}}{q_{M}}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{q_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{q_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}}\>.

The last line uses that since
\beta_{M}\geq 1
and
q_{M}\leq 1
,
\beta_{M}/q_{M}\geq 1
.
We transform that last expression to exhibit the Kullback-Leibler divergence between multinomial distributions.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L\in\mathcal{S}_{q})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{q_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{M}}\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(\frac{q_{j}}{\beta_{j}/n}\right)^{\beta_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(\frac{\beta_{j}}{n}\right)^{\beta_{j}-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{M}}\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(\frac{\beta_{j}}{n}\right)^{\beta_{j}-1}\exp\left(-n\mathrm{KL}(\frac{\beta}{n},q)\right)\>.

We now simplify the factor in front of the exponential.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{M}}\frac{\Gamma(n)}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Gamma(\beta_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(\frac{\beta_{j}}{n}\right)^{\beta_{j}-1}=\frac{1}{n}\frac{n!}{\prod_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}!}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\left(\frac{\beta_{j}}{n}\right)^{\beta_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{\beta_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}!}\>.

We use the following bound on the Stirling approximation of the factorial:
\frac{n!}{\sqrt{2\pi n}(n/e)^{n}}\in[1,2]
for all
n\geq 1
. A tighter approximation is possible, but this one is sufficient for our purpose.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\frac{n!}{n^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{\beta_{j}^{\beta_{j}}}{\beta_{j}!}\geq\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{2\pi n}e^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi\beta_{j}}e^{\beta_{j}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{2\pi n}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi\beta_{j}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sqrt{2\pi n}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi n/M}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\>.

∎

Lemma
66
links
\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}
with
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{\pm}
.

Lemma 66
.

Let
F
be a multinomial distribution supported on points
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}\in[0,B]
and let
p\in\triangle_{M}
be the corresponding probability vector.

If there exists
i\in[M]
with
X_{i}=B
and
p_{i}>0
, then for all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)=\inf_{q\in\triangle_{M}:q^{\top}X\geq\mu}\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}(p,q)\>.

If there exists
i\in[M]
with
X_{i}=0
and
p_{i}>0
, then for all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)=\inf_{q\in\triangle_{M}:q^{\top}X\leq\mu}\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}(p,q)\>.

Proof.

As remarked in
[
18
]
, the probability measure that realizes the infimum (which exists by Lemma
41
) in the
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
problem for distributions with finite support has mass on the same points and on
B
.
Under the hypothesis that there exists
i\in[M]
with
X_{i}=B
and
p_{i}>0
, we get that that infimum is also a multinomial with same support. Hence there exists
q_{F}
such that
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)=\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}(p,q_{F})
and we get
\inf_{q\in\triangle_{M}:q^{\top}X\geq\mu}\mathrm{KL}_{\mathcal{M}}(p,q)\leq\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)
.
The reverse inequality comes from the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
as an infimum over all probability distributions (which is a larger set than the multinomial distributions).

The proof for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}
is similar.
∎

Theorem 7
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}\in[0,B]
with
X_{M}=B
and let
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{M}
with
\beta_{M}>0
. Define
n=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}
. Let
F_{n}
be the multinomial distributions over the
X_{i}
with weights
\beta/n
. For all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n},\mu)\right)\>.

Proof.

If
\beta_{i}>0
for all
i\in[M]
, this is the result of a supremum over the lower bounds of Theorem
6
, to which we apply the equality of Lemma
66
. Now if there are some
i
for which
\beta_{i}=0
, we have for some
M_{0}<M
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\geq\mu)\geq\frac{M_{0}^{M_{0}/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M_{0}-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M_{0}+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n},\mu)\right)\>.

But since the leading factor is non-increasing in
M_{0}
, we recover the result with
M
instead of
M_{0}
.
∎

Corollary 3
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}\in[0,B]
with
X_{M}=0
and let
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{M}
with
\beta_{M}>0
. Define
n=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}
. Let
F_{n}
be the multinomial distributions over the
X_{i}
with weights
\beta/n
. For all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\leq\mu)\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n},\mu)\right)\>.

Proof.

Remark that
\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}X\leq\mu)=\mathbb{P}_{L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(\beta)}(L^{\top}(B-X)\geq B-\mu)
and apply Theorem
7
and Lemma
38
.
∎

G.3.2
Lower bound for bounded distributions

Lemma 67
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in[0,B]
.
Let
\theta=L^{\top}X
, where
L
is a Dirichlet random variables, with
L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1,\ldots,1)
(
n
ones).
Let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{M}\in[0,B]
, among which are the values 0 and
B
. For all
i
, let
X_{i}^{+}=\min\{Y_{k}\mid k\in[M],Y_{k}\geq X_{i}\}
. Let
F_{n}^{+}
be the empirical distribution corresponding to points
X_{i}^{+}
.

If
0\in\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
, then for all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta\leq\mu)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F_{n}^{+},\mu)\right)\>.

If
B\in\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
, then for all
\mu\in[0,B]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta\geq\mu)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F_{n}^{-},\mu)\right)\>.

Proof.

We have
\theta=L^{\top}X\leq L^{\top}X^{+}
and
\theta=L^{\top}X\geq L^{\top}X^{-}
. Those scalar products can be written as scalar products of Dirichlet random variables with
Y
.
We now apply Theorem
7
and its corollary.
∎

For a probability distribution with cdf
F
on
[0,B]
and points
0=x_{0}<x_{1}<\ldots<x_{M}<x_{M+1}=B
we define two discretized distributions with cdf given by, for
x\in[x_{m},x_{m+1})
,

\displaystyle F^{-}(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{y\to x_{m+1},y\leq x_{m+1}}F(y)\>,

\displaystyle F^{+}(x)

\displaystyle=F(x_{m})\>.

Lemma 68
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
and all probability distributions
F
supported on
[0,B]
, there exists a discretization over at most
2+\lfloor 1/\varepsilon\rfloor
points (counting points
0
and
B
) such that
\|F^{-}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
and
\|F^{+}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
M=\lfloor 1/\varepsilon\rfloor
. For
m\in\{0,\ldots,M\}
, let
x_{m}=\inf\{x\in[0,B]\mid F(x)\geq m\varepsilon\}
. Let
x_{M+1}=B
.

\displaystyle\|F^{-}-F\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{0\leq m\leq M}|\lim_{y\to x_{m+1},y\leq x_{m+1}}F(y)-F(x_{m})|\mathds{1}\{x_{m+1}\neq x_{m}\}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{0\leq m\leq M}|(m+1)\varepsilon-m\varepsilon|\leq\varepsilon\>.

The computation for
F^{+}
is similar.
∎

Lemma 69
.

For all
F,G\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
with support in
[0,B]
and all finite discretizations,

\displaystyle\|F^{-}-G^{-}\|_{\infty}\leq\|F-G\|_{\infty}\>,

\displaystyle\|F^{+}-G^{+}\|_{\infty}\leq\|F-G\|_{\infty}\>.

Proof.

For all
x\in[x_{m},x_{m+1})
,
F^{-}(x)=\lim_{y\to x_{m+1},y\leq x_{m+1}}F(y)
.

\displaystyle\|F^{-}-G^{-}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{0\leq m\leq M}|\lim_{y\to x_{m+1},y\leq x_{m+1}}F(y)-\lim_{y\to x_{m+1},y\leq x_{m+1}}G(y)|\leq\|F-G\|_{\infty}\>.

∎

Lemma 70
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
with support in
[0,B]
.
For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a discretization of
[0,B]
into
2+\lfloor 2/\varepsilon\rfloor
points such that for all
G
with
\|G-F\|\leq\varepsilon/2
, we have
\|G^{-}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
and
\|G^{+}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. For a discretization of
F
verifying the result of Lemma
68
for
\varepsilon/2
,

\displaystyle\|G^{-}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\|G^{-}-F^{-}\|_{\infty}+\|F^{-}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\|G-F\|_{\infty}+\|F^{-}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon\>.

Same computation for
G^{+}
.
∎

Lemma
71
gives a tight lower bound on the BCP for bounded distributions.

Lemma 71
.

Let
a>0
and
b<B
.
Let
F
be a probability distribution with support in
[0,B]
.

For points
X,\ldots,X_{n}\in[0,B]
. let
\theta=L^{\top}X
, where
L
is a Dirichlet random variable, with
L\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1,\ldots,1)
(
n
ones).
Let
F_{n}
be the empirical distribution corresponding to points
(X_{i})_{i\in[n]}
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
such that for all such empirical distributions (and in particular for all
n
), if
\|F_{n}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
then for all
u\in[a,b]
,

\displaystyle\text{if }B\in\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}\text{ then }\mathbb{P}(\theta\geq u)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F,\mu)-n\varepsilon\right)\>,

\displaystyle\text{if }0\in\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}\text{ then }\mathbb{P}(\theta\leq u)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{M^{M/2}}{2(8\pi)^{\frac{M-1}{2}}}\frac{1}{n^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-n\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)-n\varepsilon\right)\>.

with
M=2+\lfloor 2/\eta\rfloor
.

Proof.

The function
(F,\mu)\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)
is continuous on
\mathcal{D}\times(0,B)
by Theorem
4
. The function
(F,\mu)\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)
is then uniformly continuous on
\mathcal{D}\times[a,b]
(since
\mathcal{D}
is compact).
In particular, there exists
\eta>0
such that if
\|G-F\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
then for all
\mu\in[a,b]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(G,\mu)\leq\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F,\mu)+\varepsilon\>.

We have a similar property for
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}
.

We now build a discretization such that
F_{n}^{+}
and
F_{n}^{-}
verify that condition under the hypothesis
\|F_{n}^{(1)}-F^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
and
\|F_{n}-F\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
, using Lemma
70
. The result is a combination of this continuity inequality and Lemma
67
.
∎

Theorem
8
gives a tight lower bound on probabilities of the form
\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})
for bounded distributions.

Theorem 8
.

Let
F^{(1)}
and
F^{(2)}
be two probability distributions with means in
(0,B)
.

For points
X_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,X_{n_{1}}^{(1)},X_{1}^{(2)},\ldots,X_{n_{2}}^{(2)}\in[0,B]
such that
X_{1}^{(1)}=0,X_{n_{2}}^{(2)}=B
,
let
\theta_{1}=(L^{(1)})^{\top}X^{(1)}
and
\theta_{2}=(L^{(2)})^{\top}X^{(2)}
, where
L^{(1)}
and
L^{(2)}
are independent Dirichlet random variables, with
L^{(1)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1,\ldots,1)
(
n_{1}
ones) and
L^{(2)}\sim\mathrm{Dir}(1,\ldots,1)
(
n_{2}
ones).
Let
F_{n}^{(1)}
be the empirical distribution corresponding to points
X_{i}^{(1)}
and
F_{n}^{(2)}
be the empirical distribution corresponding to points
X_{i}^{(2)}
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta>0
such that for all such empirical distributions (and in particular for all
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
), if
\|F_{n}^{(1)}-F^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
and
\|F_{n}^{(2)}-F^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\leq\eta
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{M^{M}}{4(8\pi)^{M-1}}\frac{1}{(n_{1}n_{2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}}\exp\left(-\inf_{\mu\in[0,B]}\left(n_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F^{(1)},\mu)+n_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{(2)},\mu)\right)-(n_{1}+n_{2})\varepsilon\right)

with
M=2+\lfloor 2/\eta\rfloor
.

Proof.

By Lemma
64
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\theta_{1})\geq\sup_{\mu\in[\mu_{2},\mu_{1}]}\mathbb{P}(\theta_{2}\geq\mu)\mathbb{P}(\theta_{1}\leq\mu)

We now use Lemma
71
for both probabilities. This is valid since by hypothesis the interval
[\mu_{2},\mu_{1}]
is a subset of
(0,B)
. We get the wanted result, except that the infimum is over
\mu\in[\mu_{2},\mu_{1}]
instead of
\mu\in[0,B]
. But by the monotonicity of
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{\pm}
in
\mu
and the fact that
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{(2)},\mu)
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F^{(1)},\mu)
) is 0 for
\mu\leq\mu_{2}
(resp. for
\mu\geq\mu_{1}
), we get that

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in[0,B]}\left(n_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F^{(1)},\mu)+n_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{(2)},\mu)\right)=\inf_{\mu\in[\mu_{2},\mu_{1}]}\left(n_{1}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{-}(F^{(1)},\mu)+n_{2}\mathcal{K}_{\inf}^{+}(F^{(2)},\mu)\right)

and the theorem is proved.
∎

Appendix H
Single parameter exponential families

In this section, we explain how our analysis can be used to analyze Top Two algorithms for Single Parameter Exponential Families (SPEF). More precisely, our results apply to SPEF of sub-exponential distributions.
